
❯ # Role and context
  You are helping me complete a take-home exercise. Your job is to plan and
  build the application described below, in this repository, working with me.
  Read `docs/exercise_brief.docx` (the original brief) and the inputs in
  `data/input/` before doing anything else.

  You own the complete planning of this project: the analysis of the problem,
  the choice of architecture, framework, LLM and tooling, the project
  structure, the milestones and the test strategy. I will not hand you a plan.
  I will review your proposals and make the final call at the decision points
  listed in the process below.

  Everything must be in English: code comments, docstrings, documentation,
  commit messages, UI text, reports and our conversation.

  # The task (summary of the brief)
  Build a small working application for a fictional vehicle controller
  (VCU-42). Engineers keep specifications in Word and tests in Excel. The app
  must let an engineer:
  1. See what changed between two spec versions (`VCU_Spec_Baseline.docx`,
  `VCU_Spec_Revised.docx`).
  2. See which tests in `VCU_Test_Register.xlsx` may be affected, and review an
  explanation of why.
  3. Make or upload a further small change and see the updated result in the
  UI.
  4. Export a readable engineering impact summary (PDF, HTML or Markdown),
  accessible from the UI.

  Hard requirements:
  - An LLM must be a meaningful part of the analysis, not just a restatement of
  a final result.
  - A clear UI: a reviewer must understand the change, the affected items and
  the result without reading code.
  - Never commit secrets. Model configuration goes through environment
  variables (`.env` is git-ignored, `.env.example` is committed).
  - Deliverables: README.md (what it does, install and run with exact commands,
  safe model configuration, how to reproduce a result, assumptions, link to a
  sample report), a screenshot or short demo, and an example exported report
  committed to the repo. The repo must be runnable by a reviewer.

  # What I know about the data (verify it yourself, do not trust me blindly)
  - The only substantive spec change appears to be VCU-DR-01: response time 100
  ms -> 80 ms. Verify this by diffing, and make the diff generic so it works
  for any change.
  - The test register is deliberately messy: some tests have no requirement
  link, some have "TBD", one links to a requirement that does not exist
  (VCU-OLD-88), results are free text such as "PASS (94 ms)", and a second
  sheet of workshop notes contains operational warnings (for example, brake
  latency and torque latency are measured on different clocks and must not be
  conflated; a past PASS may not apply to a newer requirement; final approval
  belongs to the authorized owner).
  - Treat the register as evidence, not approval. Reports must give
  recommendations and never claim approval.

  # Design guidance
  - Use deterministic code for what is deterministic (parsing .docx and .xlsx,
  extracting requirements by ID, diffing, structured data models). Use the LLM
  for what needs semantic judgment (which tests are relevant to a changed
  requirement even without a valid link, ambiguous or missing links,
  interpreting free-text results and thresholds against the new value,
  explaining the reasoning).
  - Do not rely only on requirement-ID matching: the register has missing, TBD
  and orphaned links.
  - Every test should end with a status, a confidence level and a short
  evidence-based reason, including tests judged not affected. Use a small,
  clear set of statuses (for example: directly affected, possibly affected,
  needs human review, not affected).
  - Use structured LLM outputs (validated JSON schema) so the UI and report are
  built from reliable data.
  - Make the LLM provider configurable (provider, model, base URL, key via
  environment variables). Prefer a design that runs without any key, for
  example a cached or mock mode that replays a saved real analysis, so a
  reviewer can run the app without credentials. Be explicit in the README about
  what is real LLM output and what is replayed.
  - Keep it simple. This is a first working version: no authentication, no
  database server, no over-engineering. Prefer a small, well-organized codebase
  with a few tests for the parsing and diffing logic.

  # Process: you plan, I decide
  Do NOT start coding immediately. Work in this order, and wait for my approval
  at each numbered step:

  1. **Understand**: read all files in the repo, then summarize in your own
  words the task, the data and the risks you see. Flag anything in my
  description above that you believe is wrong.
  2. **LLM selection**: my main objective is a RELIABLE LLM for this task,
  since the quality of the analysis depends on it. I have NO hosted API key.
  Evaluate realistic options, including (but not limited to) a local
  open-weight model via Ollama, a free-tier hosted API and a replay or cache
  mode. Do not discard any option up front, Ollama included: I know a local
  model can be heavy on my machine, but it may still be the right choice
  depending on the trade-offs. Compare the options on cost, time (setup and
  per-run latency), performance and reliability for this specific task
  (structured output, reasoning over messy engineering text), hardware
  requirements, reproducibility for a reviewer, and setup effort. Where my
  hardware matters, give me exact Ubuntu commands to check it. Give a clear
  recommendation with your reasoning, then wait for my choice.
  3. **UI and framework**: compare Streamlit and FastAPI (with a simple
  frontend) for this task, considering development speed, UI quality, how well
  each supports a clear diff view, an impact table with drill-down, file upload
  and editing, report export, testability, and ease of running for a reviewer.
  Give a recommendation, then wait for my choice.
  4. **Plan**: you produce the full plan: project structure, data flow from the
  input files to the report, data models, prompt strategy, test strategy and a
  short milestone list. Keep it small and justify the main choices. Wait for
  my approval.
  5. **Build** in small, working increments, committing each one with a clear
  message. Run the app and the tests yourself, and show me results as you go.
  6. **Packaging**: my development environment is Ubuntu (Python virtual
  environment, ideally with `uv`, and a Makefile for common commands). The
  submission must also run on Windows or in a container, so add a Dockerfile
  and exact commands in the README. Do not claim something runs on Windows or
  in Docker unless you actually verified it, or say clearly that it was not
  verified.
  7. **Deliverables**: README, example exported report committed under a clear
  folder, a screenshot or demo of the running UI, and a clean `.gitignore`
  check that no secrets are tracked.

  Step 1: Understanding

  The task in my words

  Engineers want to know what a spec revision breaks in their verification
  evidence without reading everything by hand. The app has to:
  - compare two Word specs requirement by requirement;
  - assess every test in a messy Excel register against the changes, with a
    status, a confidence level and a reason based on evidence;
  - let the engineer change a spec or upload a new one and re-run;
  - export a report.

  The LLM's job is to judge things the code can't decide with certainty: tests
  with no link, results written as free text, and thresholds compared against
  the new value. The report gives recommendations only. It never approves
  anything.

  The data

  Specs. Both have the same layout. Each requirement is a bold teal paragraph
  VCU-XX-NN␠␠Title followed by one paragraph of body text. There are six
  requirements (DR-01, DR-02, BRK-03, DGN-04, LOG-05, MOD-06), plus a metadata
  table and context sections. There are no tracked changes or comments, and the
  headers, footers and core properties are identical. The full text diff has
  four changes:

  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Location    │ Baseline (Rev A) │           Revised (Rev B)            │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Subtitle +    │ Revision A |     │                                      │
  │ "Document     │ baseline         │ Revision B | candidate change        │
  │ state"        │                  │                                      │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ VCU-DR-01     │ within 100       │ within 80 milliseconds               │
  │ body          │ milliseconds     │                                      │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ "Initial         │ "Customer requested tighter          │
  │ §4 Revision   │ validation       │ response-time objective… test        │
  │ notes         │ baseline…"       │ procedures and evidence have not yet │
  │               │                  │  been reapproved"                    │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  Register, sheet "Test register". 12 tests (TV-001 to TV-012). The header row
  is row 5, not row 1, with three title rows above it. There are no merged
  cells, hidden rows or hyperlinks.

  Sheet "Workshop notes". 5 notes. Three are your warnings. The other two matter
  as well:
  - "TBD is not validated."
  - "The high-load campaign lost requirement hyperlinks after migration." This
    points straight at TV-003.

  Corrections and additions to your description

  1. It's not quite true that DR-01 is the only change. It is the only change to
     a requirement, but the revision label and the §4 Revision notes also
     changed. The new notes say evidence "has not yet been reapproved", which
     supports flagging old PASS results. The diff should show these as
     non-requirement or document-level changes, not hide them.
  2. Multi-valued links. TV-002 has VCU-DR-01; VCU-BRK-03, so link parsing must
     split lists.
  3. Results are more varied than "PASS (94 ms)". There are also PASS, PASS
     (trend) and NOT RUN. TV-007 has no last-run date.
  4. Missing from your summary: the brief (v3.0) makes a share link to the AI
     conversation mandatory. It must be a read-only URL to the original
     conversation; screenshots and reconstructed logs are explicitly not
     accepted. Before we invest time here, please check that the tool you're
     using can produce such a link. I'm not certain this VS Code session can
     produce a public share URL. If it can't, you may need a separate shareable
     chat for the substantive work.
  5. Windows or container. The brief asks for one or the other; you're asking
     for both, which is fine. I'm running on WSL2, so I can verify Ubuntu and
     probably Docker, but not native Windows. I'll document Windows as not
     verified unless you test it.

  Risks

  - LLM reliability:
    - conflating numbers (88 ms brake against 80 ms torque; 80% pedal against 80
      ms);
    - inventing requirement IDs;
    - skipping tests;
    - inconsistent results between runs;
    - approval language slipping in.

    Mitigations:
    - the code pre-computes facts (link validity, parsed ms values, measured
      quantity);
    - every output is validated against a schema;
    - a check confirms all 12 tests are present and every cited ID exists;
    - temperature 0;
    - a scan for approval wording.
  - Generic diff on uploaded files. A docx edited in Word may split runs, lose
    the teal formatting, have bodies spanning several paragraphs, or contain
    renamed or re-ID'd requirements. Detection should key on the ID pattern
    rather than on styling, and handle added, removed, modified and unchanged
    requirements.
  - Replay mode only covers inputs it has seen. A cached analysis is valid only
    for exactly those inputs. For a new edit without a model, we have to decide
    what happens: refuse, or run a clearly labelled deterministic fallback. This
    belongs in step 2.
  - Local environment. uv and python-docx aren't installed yet. This doesn't
    block anything; it's just setup.

  Questions before step 2

  1. "Make or upload a further small change": I plan to support both editing
     requirement text in the UI and uploading a replacement spec .docx. Do you
     agree?
  2. Should the engineer also be able to upload a modified test register (for
     example, a test added or a link fixed)? My default is yes: upload only, no
     in-app editing of the register.
  3. How will you handle the mandatory conversation share link?

  Once you've confirmed (or corrected) the above, I'll move to step 2, the LLM
  selection, starting with the commands to check your hardware.

● Step 2: LLM selection

  Your hardware (checked read-only on this machine)

  Item: CPU

  What it means: Good for running a model on the CPU

  What it means: Limits the model size unless you raise the cap
  ────────────────────────────────────────

  What it means: In WSL, Ollama will very likely run CPU-only (ROCm doesn't

  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Item: Tools

  What it means: All need installing

  The commands to re-check this yourself on Ubuntu:
  lscpu | grep -E 'Model name|^CPU\(s\)'          # CPU model and thread count
  grep -o -m1 'avx512f\|avx2' /proc/cpuinfo        # SIMD support (speeds up CPU
  inference)
  free -h                                          # RAM available to Ubuntu/WSL
  df -h ~                                          # disk space for model
  weights
  nvidia-smi || lspci | grep -iE 'vga|3d'          # GPU (nvidia-smi absent = no
  NVIDIA GPU)
  powershell.exe -c "(Get-CimInstance
  Win32_ComputerSystem).TotalPhysicalMemory/1GB"  # host RAM (WSL only)

  What the task asks of the model

  Per analysis, the input is roughly 4–6k tokens: the changed requirements, 12
  tests, 5 notes and the instructions. The output is about 2–3k tokens of JSON,
  one verdict per test. The context is small, but the reasoning has to be
  careful: the model must tell brake latency on a different clock from torque
  latency, "80% pedal" from "80 ms", and say that a PASS without timing proves
  nothing. It also has to produce schema-valid JSON every time. Reasoning
  quality is the bottleneck, not context length.

  Options compared

  The latency figures for local models are my estimates for your CPU, not
  measurements.

  Option: A. Ollama, small dense model (qwen3:8b, ~5 GB)
  Cost: Free
  Setup: ~15 min
  Per-run latency: ~3–6 min on CPU
  Expected quality on the traps: Moderate. 8B models are the most likely to
    fall for the 88 ms brake / 80 ms torque trap
  Structured output: Good: Ollama enforces a JSON schema through grammar
  Hardware: Fits in 15 GiB
  Reviewer reproducibility: Poor: the reviewer must install Ollama and
    download 5 GB
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: B. Ollama, MoE model (gpt-oss:20b ~13 GB, or qwen3:30b-a3b ~19 GB)
  Cost: Free
  Setup: ~30 min, and you must raise the WSL memory cap to ~24 GB
  Per-run latency: ~2–5 min. Only ~3B parameters are active per token, so it
    is faster than its size suggests
  Expected quality on the traps: Good: clearly better than 8B, still below
    frontier models
  Structured output: Good (grammar-enforced)
  Hardware: Needs .wslconfig memory=24GB
  Reviewer reproducibility: Poor (13–19 GB download)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: C. GitHub Models (free with a GitHub account; OpenAI GPT-4.1-class
    and others)
  Cost: Free, rate-limited per day
  Setup: ~5 min: a fine-grained PAT with models:read
  Per-run latency: ~10–40 s
  Expected quality on the traps: High
  Structured output: Strict JSON schema, the most dependable
  Hardware: None
  Reviewer reproducibility: Good: every reviewer has a GitHub account and can
    do a live run with their own token
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: D. Google Gemini API free tier (Flash-class)
  Cost: Free, rate-limited
  Setup: ~5 min: AI Studio key
  Per-run latency: ~10–30 s
  Expected quality on the traps: High
  Structured output: Native response schema
  Hardware: None
  Reviewer reproducibility: Medium: the reviewer needs a Google key. Free-tier
    prompts may be used by Google, which is acceptable because all the data is
    fictional
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: E. Groq / Cerebras / Mistral free tiers (Llama-3.3-70B,
    gpt-oss-120b, Mistral)
  Cost: Free, rate-limited
  Setup: ~5 min (Mistral asks for phone verification)
  Per-run latency: ~3–20 s
  Expected quality on the traps: High (70–120B models)
  Structured output: Good; strict mode varies by model
  Hardware: None
  Reviewer reproducibility: Medium
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: F. OpenRouter :free models
  Cost: Free
  Setup: ~5 min
  Per-run latency: Variable
  Expected quality on the traps: Variable
  Structured output: Varies by upstream provider
  Hardware: None
  Reviewer reproducibility: Low: models appear and disappear
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: G. Replay / cache
  Cost: Free
  Setup: None
  Per-run latency: Instant
  Expected quality on the traps: Exactly that of the model that produced the
    recording
  Structured output: Already validated
  Hardware: None
  Reviewer reproducibility: Excellent, but only for inputs that were analysed
    before

  Model names and free-tier limits change often. I'll confirm the exact
  available model IDs and limits when we set up the provider, not from memory.

  Two more possibilities I noticed:
  - Databricks: this machine has Databricks CLI profiles. If that's an employer
    workspace, I'd advise against using it. The brief excludes employer
    resources, and a reviewer couldn't reproduce runs on it anyway.
  - Asking the company: the brief says "Contact us if model access would prevent
    you from starting". Asking them for a key is a legitimate option.

  Recommendation

  These pieces don't compete with each other, because the design should be
  provider-agnostic. GitHub Models, Gemini, Groq, Mistral, OpenRouter and Ollama
  all expose an OpenAI-compatible API. One client, configured with
  LLM_PROVIDER, LLM_MODEL, LLM_BASE_URL and LLM_API_KEY, covers all of them.
  That makes the choice mainly about which provider we develop against and
  record the reference run with:

  1. Primary (live analysis and the committed reference run): C, GitHub Models,
     with a GPT-4.1-class model.
     - It has the most dependable schema adherence and the strongest reasoning
       available for free.
     - It needs no new account.
     - It is the only option where a reviewer can do a live run with credentials
       they already have.

     The risk is the daily rate limits. One analysis is 1–3 calls, so that's
     fine for development and a demo. Gemini (D) is the documented drop-in
     fallback.
  2. Secondary, a supported local path: B, gpt-oss:20b via Ollama.
     - It's the only option that works offline and sends no data outside your
       machine.
     - It needs the WSL memory cap raised to about 24 GB.
     - If you'd rather not change WSL settings, use qwen3:8b instead (option A),
       accepting lower quality.
     - I'd treat it as optional, and the README would state its measured quality
       honestly.
  3. Default for reviewers without credentials: G, replay.
     - The app ships the real recorded LLM output, keyed by a hash of the inputs
       and labelled in the UI as "Replayed: recorded with model X on date".
     - For a new edit with no model configured, the app still runs the
       deterministic part (diff, link checks, ms values extracted from results).
       It clearly marks the LLM assessment as "not run" and never invents one.
  4. Reliability by measurement, not by claim.
     - I'd turn my expected-outcome table from step 1 into a small evaluation
       set: the expected status of each test, with emphasis on TV-001, 003, 004,
       007 and 012.
     - The same prompt runs against each provider we try.
     - The README reports the scores. That gives you evidence for "reliable" and
       a good topic for the follow-up interview.

  Your decisions

  1. Primary provider: GitHub Models (recommended), Gemini, or something else?
  2. Local Ollama path:
     - (a) include it with gpt-oss:20b, and you raise the WSL memory cap (I'd
       give you the exact .wslconfig lines; it's a Windows-side file, so you'd
       apply it yourself);
     - (b) include it with qwen3:8b, with no WSL change;
     - (c) skip it, keep only the OpenAI-compatible abstraction, and document
       Ollama as "should work, not verified".
  3. Evaluation set: do you agree with adding the small evaluation set and the
     bake-off?

● Evaluation set: custom, not an established benchmark

  I recommend a custom dataset built from the project's own files. Established
  benchmarks are still useful as a quick filter, but they answer a different
  question:

  What it measures
  Established benchmark (e.g. IFEval, JSONSchemaBench, MMLU): General ability:
    following instructions, valid JSON, broad knowledge
  Custom dataset from our files: Our exact task: messy links, free-text
    results, the different-clock trap, tightening vs loosening a limit
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Results available
  Established benchmark (e.g. IFEval, JSONSchemaBench, MMLU): Already
    published for most models, so running it ourselves adds little
  Custom dataset from our files: Doesn't exist anywhere; we'd produce it
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Cost
  Established benchmark (e.g. IFEval, JSONSchemaBench, MMLU): Hundreds to
    thousands of calls, more than the free-tier limits
  Custom dataset from our files: About 50 calls per model
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Weakness
  Established benchmark (e.g. IFEval, JSONSchemaBench, MMLU): Doesn't tell us
    whether a model confuses 88 ms brake latency with 80 ms torque latency
  Custom dataset from our files: The labels are mine (a single annotator) and
    the dataset is small

  In the report I'd cite published benchmark scores as context only. The custom
  set is what we decide on.

  Proposed dataset: 7 change scenarios × 12 tests = 84 labelled judgments

  Each scenario is the baseline spec plus one generated edit. The register stays
  the original file, so all the messiness is kept. These scenarios also cover
  the kind of "small change" a reviewer might ask you to demo.

  #: S1
  Change: DR-01 100 → 80 ms (the real revision)
  What it tests: The baseline case
  Key expected labels: TV-001 directly affected; TV-003 directly or possibly
    affected despite no link; TV-004 not affected (different clock); TV-007
    needs review ("80%" is not "80 ms"); TV-012 not affected
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S2
  Change: BRK-03 gains a limit of "within 80 ms"
  What it tests: The reverse of the clock trap
  Key expected labels: TV-004 directly affected (88 ms); TV-001 not affected
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S3
  Change: DR-02 spike filter 10 → 5 ms
  What it tests: Reasoning about the direction of a non-latency limit
  Key expected labels: TV-010 directly affected: a 7 ms pulse is no longer
    "shorter than" the limit, so the expected outcome of the test is now wrong
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S4
  Change: DR-01 100 → 120 ms (relaxed)
  What it tests: Over-flagging when a limit is loosened
  Key expected labels: TV-001's evidence still holds; at most possibly
    affected, because the script still has 100 ms
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S5
  Change: DGN-04 reworded, same meaning
  What it tests: Over-flagging a cosmetic change
  Key expected labels: TV-005 not affected or possibly affected
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S6
  Change: MOD-06 removed
  What it tests: A link that becomes orphaned
  Key expected labels: TV-009 needs human review
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S7
  Change: New requirement VCU-THM-07 (thermal torque derating)
  What it tests: An added requirement with no existing links
  Key expected labels: TV-007 possibly affected or needs review; others mostly
    not affected

  Ambiguous cases get a set of acceptable statuses, not a single answer. I'll
  write the labels to a file (eval/labels.yaml) for you to review before any
  run. You're the engineer; I don't want the score to depend only on my
  judgment.

  Metrics

  - Schema validity: valid on the first attempt, and after one automatic retry.
  - Coverage: all 12 tests returned, no invented test or requirement IDs.
  - Status accuracy against the acceptable sets.
  - Missed impacts: a test whose true label is directly affected but which the
    model marks not affected. This is the most serious error, so I report it
    separately.
  - Trap score: the named traps above (TV-004 in S1, TV-001 in S2, TV-010 in S3,
    TV-007, TV-012).
  - Approval-language violations: a regex check for "approved", "certified" and
    similar.
  - Consistency: S1 runs 3 times per model and we count how often the statuses
    flip.
  - Latency and token counts.
  - Quality of the reasons: I'll spot-check a sample by hand and quote it. I
    won't use an LLM as the judge, to keep things simple and avoid a
    self-grading loop.

  The same prompt and schema go to every model, with no tuning for any one
  model, at temperature 0. That's about 9 calls per model and about 54 in total,
  which fits within the free tiers. For qwen3:8b on CPU I estimate 30–50
  minutes in total, and I'll run it in the background.

  Report

  docs/llm_evaluation.md will contain:
  - the step 2 analysis: hardware, the options table (cost, setup, latency,
    hardware, reproducibility) and the argument for one provider-agnostic
    client;
  - the method and labels;
  - the results tables;
  - worked examples of failures;
  - a final recommendation.

  One process point

  The evaluation needs a provisional prompt, an output schema and a minimal spec
  parser and diff, which is real code written before we decide steps 3 and 4.
  I'd build it as a self-contained eval/ spike plus a small shared core (parser,
  diff, schema, prompt) that the app will reuse. That way nothing is thrown
  away, and the evaluation also tests the actual pipeline. It doesn't commit us
  to Streamlit or FastAPI.

  What you need to do (I can't create accounts)

  1. Create free keys and put them in a local .env that you edit yourself. .env
  is already in .gitignore. I'll never print or log it.
  GITHUB_TOKEN=...      # github.com/settings/personal-access-tokens →
  fine-grained, permission "Models: read"
  GEMINI_API_KEY=...    # aistudio.google.com/apikey
  GROQ_API_KEY=...      # console.groq.com/keys
  CEREBRAS_API_KEY=...  # cloud.cerebras.ai → API keys
  MISTRAL_API_KEY=...   # console.mistral.ai → free "Experiment" plan (asks for
  phone verification)
  Whichever key is missing, that provider is simply skipped and marked "not run"
  in the report.

  2. Install Ollama and the model. Pick one of the two installs:
  # (a) official installer (needs sudo, sets up a systemd service)
  curl -fsSL https://ollama.com/install.sh | sh
  # (b) user-space alternative, no sudo
  mkdir -p ~/.local/ollama && curl -fL
  https://ollama.com/download/ollama-linux-amd64.tgz | tar -xz -C
  ~/.local/ollama
  ~/.local/ollama/bin/ollama serve &   # then in another shell:
  ollama pull qwen3:8b                 # ~5 GB

  3. Let me install uv. It installs user-level to ~/.local/bin.

  I'll choose one current model per provider from each provider's live /models
  list at run time, roughly: GPT-4.1-class on GitHub, Flash-class on Gemini,
  gpt-oss-120b or Llama-3.3-70B on Groq and Cerebras, Mistral Medium or Large,
  and qwen3:8b. The exact IDs go in the report.

  Please confirm:
  1. The custom dataset and the 7 scenarios. Add or drop any?
  2. Building it as an eval/ spike plus a shared core.
  3. Go ahead with installing uv.

  After that I'll write the labels for you to review while you create the keys.

● The evaluation harness is built. No model has been run yet: there are no keys
  and Ollama isn't installed. Here is what's in place and what's needed to start
  the runs.

  What's built

  - Shared core in src/vcu_impact/. The app will reuse it as-is:
    - spec_parser.py finds requirements by their ID pattern, not by styling.
    - diff.py is generic: added, removed and modified requirements, title
      changes, numeric changes paired by unit, a word-level diff, and changes
      outside the requirements.
    - register_parser.py locates the header row by column names. It classifies
      each link as valid, removed in revision, unknown, TBD or missing, and
      extracts values and ranges such as "75–95%".
    - spec_editor.py applies edits to a .docx while keeping its formatting.
    - llm.py is the provider-agnostic client:
      - every hosted provider goes through its OpenAI-compatible endpoint with a
        strict JSON schema;
      - it falls back to JSON mode if a provider rejects the schema;
      - Pydantic validates the output, with one repair retry;
      - Ollama uses its native API, so the context window can be set (its
        default is too small for our prompt).
    - prompt.py and analysis.py:
      - the post-checks guarantee all 12 tests are covered, strip invented
        requirement IDs and flag approval wording;
      - the code never invents an assessment: a missing one is filled as
        needs_human_review with a visible warning.
  - Evaluation in eval/:
    - dataset.yaml holds the scenarios and labels. Please review the labels,
      especially the accept sets.
    - models.yaml lists the model IDs. They're still provisional; I'll confirm
      them against each provider's live list.
    - evaluate.py has four commands: build, list-models, run (resumable, and
      stops a provider cleanly on a rate limit) and score.
    - The 6 generated scenario .docx files are in eval/scenarios/. Each was
      re-parsed and diffed, and shows exactly the intended change.
  - 11 unit tests pass, and ruff lint and format are clean. The prompt is about
    3.3k input tokens.
  - .env.example lists the LLM_* variables and the per-provider keys. .env
    doesn't exist yet, and I confirmed it's git-ignored.

  To start the runs, I need:

  1. Your keys in .env: cp .env.example .env, then fill in GITHUB_TOKEN,
     GEMINI_API_KEY, GROQ_API_KEY, CEREBRAS_API_KEY and MISTRAL_API_KEY. The
     signup links are in my previous message. I won't open or print the file;
     the code only reads it.
  2. Ollama. Either you run the official installer (it needs sudo), or I do the
     no-sudo install into ~/.local/ollama and pull qwen3:8b. That's roughly a
     1–2 GB binary plus a 5 GB model. Which do you prefer?
  3. Commits. Nothing is committed yet. Should I commit directly to main (simple
     for a take-home repo) or on a feature branch with a merge at each
     milestone? I'd suggest main with small commits.

  Once the keys are set, I'll run list-models, fix the model IDs, run the full
  evaluation, and write docs/llm_evaluation.md with your step 2 analysis, the
  method, the results and the recommendation.

  - Every model avoided the main traps:
    - TV-004 (brake latency on a different clock) and its reverse case;
    - "80% pedal" vs "80 ms";
    - TV-012 start-up timing;
    - the unlinked TV-003 was flagged.

    the only labeller; the report says so.

  What went differently from my step-2 plan

  - GitHub Models was retired on 30 July 2026. My earlier recommendation of it
    came from outdated knowledge. Its endpoint now answers "OK" to every
    request, even unauthenticated ones.
  - Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite was unavailable for over an hour ("high demand"
    errors), so I left it out of the ranking with one completed run. Cerebras
    and Mistral weren't run, since you reported they required a paid account.
  - The test harness had two bugs, now fixed. Both runs it affected were re-run;
    the superseded output is kept in eval/results/_superseded/.
    - Groq's default output limit cut off long answers, and the harness misread
      that as "schema not supported". The client now sets an explicit output
      budget.
    - Groq's own rejection of a generated answer made the call fail outright. It
      now goes through the normal repair retry.
  - Two of my labels were wrong, not the models. In S4 and S7, the strong models
    said "directly affected" because the script or procedure must be updated,
    even though the old evidence still holds. That matches my own prompt's
    definition. I didn't change the labels after seeing results; the report
    shows a separate score with those two cells accepted, where all three strong
    models reach 16/16.

  Decisions for you

  1. Which LLM? My recommendation:
     - primary: Groq qwen3.8-27b, the most accurate on critical cases, fully
       consistent and fast enough to re-analyse live in a demo;
     - fallback: Gemini 3.5 Flash, the most dependable output in this run but
       1–6 minutes per analysis;
     - local qwen3:8b documented as an offline option, not the default;
     - replay of recorded runs for reviewers without keys.

     procedure text only". I suggest adding an explicit required action field to

❯ explain better the point 2: Status design. One status can't tell "re-run the
  test" from "update the procedure text only". I suggest adding an explicit
  required action field to each result: re-run, update procedure, fix link, or
  none. Do you agree?

● Right now one status tries to answer two different questions. Adding an action
  field would separate them.

  The problem: one field, two meanings

  The status says how strongly the test is tied to the change. It doesn't say
  what the engineer has to do about it. In the evaluation, the models answered
  directly_affected for tests that need very different work:

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Case         │ Status the  │ What the engineer actually has to  │
  │                     │ models gave │                 do                 │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ S1 TV-001: limit    │ directly    │ Re-run. The old evidence no longer │
  │ 100 → 80 ms, old    │ affected    │  meets the new limit, and the      │
  │ result 94 ms        │             │ script threshold must change too.  │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ S4 TV-001: limit    │             │ Edit the script and procedure text │
  │ 100 → 120 ms, old   │ directly    │  only. 94 ms still meets 120 ms,   │
  │ result 94 ms        │ affected    │ so the old evidence holds and no   │
  │                     │             │ re-run is needed.                  │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ S1 TV-003: no       │ directly    │ Fix the link to VCU-DR-01, then    │
  │ requirement link,   │ affected    │ re-run against 80 ms.              │
  │ 92 ms               │             │                                    │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ S1 TV-002: linked   │ possibly    │ Re-run with timing captured. The   │
  │ to DR-01 but no     │ affected    │ old PASS says nothing about 80 ms. │
  │ timing recorded     │             │                                    │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ S6 TV-009: its only │ directly    │ Re-link or retire the test. There  │
  │  requirement was    │ affected    │ is nothing to re-run.              │
  │ removed             │             │                                    │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  The S4 TV-001 row is the disagreement in the evaluation. Three strong models
  said directly_affected because the script must be updated. That was a correct
  judgment, but my label expected possibly_affected or not_affected. The status
  couldn't express "affected, but only the paperwork changes". The models and my
  label were answering different questions.

  The proposal

  Keep the status and add a required-actions field, a list rather than a single
  value, because TV-003 needs two actions:

  - rerun: the old evidence is invalid or missing for the new requirement.
  - update_procedure: the threshold, script or text must change; the old
    evidence may still hold.
  - fix_link: the requirement link is missing, TBD, unknown or points to a
    removed requirement.
  - owner_decision: a human has to decide, for example whether to retire the
    test or who owns it.
  - none: nothing to do.

  What this gives the UI and report

  - A work list instead of a status table. The engineer can filter for "tests to
    re-run" or "tests whose link must be fixed". The report can say "3 re-runs,
    2 procedure updates, 2 links to fix", rather than leaving the engineer to
    read free-text recommendations.
  - Cleaner scoring. I can label the action precisely: S4 TV-001 is
    update_procedure, not rerun. The label stops being ambiguous, and I can
    score the models on that distinction.
  - Less reliance on the LLM. fix_link follows directly from the parsed link
    status, so code can assign it deterministically. Deciding between rerun and
    update_procedure needs judgment: does the old value still meet the new
    limit? That part goes to the LLM, and it matches the "deterministic where
    possible" guidance in your brief.

  The cost

  The evaluation ran prompt v1, which has no action field. Adding it means a
  prompt v2 and a schema change. I'd then re-run the primary model on the 7
  scenarios and check that accuracy doesn't drop, then score the action field as
  well. On Groq that takes a few minutes. The numbers in the current report
  would be labelled as v1. The statuses themselves stay the same.

  What I need from you

  Do you agree with this? Two smaller choices go with it:
  - Action list: is the five-value list above right, or would you rather drop
    owner_decision and keep it to four?
  - fix_link: do you want it set by code, as I'd suggest, or left to the LLM
    like the rest?

  Once you've decided, I'll update the schema and prompt, re-run the primary
  model, and then commit.

● Yes, v3 finished: 9 of 9 runs succeeded, and all were valid on the first try.
  It's a mixed result. The number I'd lead with is accuracy of the status field
  on Groq qwen3.8-27b, using the same 84 judgments for v1, v2 and v3.

  ┌─────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┬────────┬────────┐
  │                         │      v1 (no actions)       │   v2   │   v3   │
  ├─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────┼────────┤
  │ Accuracy (lenient)      │ 96% (81/84), 98% with the  │ 99%    │ 99%    │
  │                         │ two relabelled cells       │ (83)   │ (83)   │
  ├─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────┼────────┤
  │ Critical cases          │ 15/16                      │ 15/16  │ 15/16  │
  ├─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────┼────────┤
  │ Missed impacts          │ 0                          │ 0      │ 0      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────┼────────┤
  │ Unneeded review flags   │ 5                          │ 10     │ 5      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────┼────────┤
  │ Required-action         │ n/a                        │ 15/16  │ 15/16  │
  │ accuracy                │                            │        │        │
  ├─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────┼────────┤
  │ Same answers on 3 S1    │ 12/12                      │ 12/12  │ 12/12  │
  │ runs                    │                            │        │        │
  ├─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────┼────────┤
  │ Strict accuracy         │ 82%                        │ 75%    │ 76%    │
  │ (preferred label)       │                            │        │        │
  └─────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┴────────┴────────┘

  - The prompt change had no measurable accuracy benefit. v1 with the two
    relabelled cells scores 82/84 and v3 scores 83/84, which is noise. The 99%
    shown comes from my relabelling, not from the new prompt.
  - v2 added review noise. It wrongly flagged unrelated tests for review (10
    flags, up from 5), and the v3 wording brought that back to 5. I didn't
    change the prompt beyond that.
  - The required-actions field works. It gets 15 of 16 labelled cases right. The
    one miss is S1 TV-002 (linked to DR-01, with no timing in the old
    evidence), where the model chose owner_decision instead of rerun.
  - One critical failure is a regression from v1. S5 TV-005 (DGN-04 reworded
    with the same meaning) is directly_affected in v2 and v3. The model's own
    reason says "the behavior is unchanged", which contradicts the status. v1
    got this case right, and my v3 wording meant to fix it but didn't.
  - Strict accuracy fell from 82% to 76%. Most of the change is unlinked tests
    such as TV-008 (the orphan link): v1 said "needs review" and v3 says "not
    affected" plus fix_link. That is the behaviour I designed, since the code
    now flags broken links itself, but my "preferred" labels weren't written for
    it. I'm not changing labels after seeing results, so treat the strict drop
    as a label/design mismatch rather than worse judgment.

  The S5 TV-005 failure is the open issue. There are two options:
  1. Fix it in code. If the model's own change interpretation says
     reworded_same_meaning and it marks a test directly_affected, the post-check
     could flag the contradiction for review rather than silently accepting it.
     This is deterministic, cheap, and doesn't need another prompt iteration.
  2. Leave it and document it. I'd list it as a known limitation in the report.

  I'd go with option 1. Either way I'll record the three versions in the report,
  with the strict-accuracy drop explained, and then commit as agreed. Which do
  you prefer?

❯ Go with option 1, write the report and then commit

  What each version represents

  The version number is the prompt version. Only v1 compared models.

  Version: v1
  What it is: Status only (directly / possibly / needs review / not affected),
    plus confidence, reason and a free-text recommendation
  Models run: 5 (Groq qwen3.8-27b, Gemini 3.5 Flash, Gemini Flash-Lite, Groq
    gpt-oss-120b, local qwen3:8b)
  Purpose: Choose the model
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Version: v2
  What it is: v1 plus required_actions (rerun, update_procedure,
    owner_decision, none), with fix_link set by the code. Status definitions
    reworded: status means relevance, the action means work
  Models run: qwen3.8-27b only
  Purpose: Test the new field
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Version: v3
  What it is: v2 with two wording changes made after reading v2's results: a
    broken link alone no longer means needs_human_review, and a pure rewording
    affects no test directly
  Models run: qwen3.8-27b only
  Purpose: Fix v2's extra review flags

  Which to use: v3, with Groq qwen3.8-27b

  Prompt v3 is a pragmatic choice, not one the evidence proves is better. Here
  is what I know:
  - v3 is the only version with the actions field. I think that field is worth
    having, and it gets 15 of 16 labelled cases right.
  - Status accuracy is no better than v1. Against the like-for-like baseline, v3
    scores 83/84 and v1 scores 82/84, which is noise.
  - It still has one real regression. S5 TV-005 (a reworded requirement) is
    wrongly directly_affected, where v1 got it right. The new post-check only
    flags that contradiction; it doesn't fix the status.
  - It's not independently validated. I tuned v3's wording on the same scenarios
    I scored it on.

  The alternative is v1 with the actions derived in code. That's cleaner on S5
  and strict accuracy, but it loses the rerun-vs-update-procedure distinction.
  I'd stay with v3 and say plainly in the README that this is the weak spot.

  For the model, I'd keep Groq qwen3.8-27b, with Gemini 3.5 Flash as the
  fallback and replay for reviewers without keys. The ranking among the top
  three is within noise, as the report says.

  Step 3: UI framework

  What the UI has to do

  - Change view: each changed requirement shown old vs new with word-level
    highlighting, plus non-requirement changes such as the revision notes.
  - Impact table: 12 tests with status, confidence and actions, colour-coded and
    filterable. Selecting a row shows the reason, the evidence, and which
    actions come from the model and which come from the code.
  - Visible warnings: post-check warnings next to the test, including the S5
    rewording contradiction.
  - Edit and re-run: edit a requirement's text in the browser (or add or remove
    one), or upload a new spec or register, then re-analyse.
  - Run status: a run takes 5 s to several minutes, so the UI needs a visible
    progress state and a clear "live" vs "replayed" label.
  - Report export from the UI.

  Comparison

  Criterion: Development speed
  Streamlit: Fast: one Python app, roughly 400–600 lines on top of the
    existing core
  FastAPI + simple frontend: Slower: an API, HTML templates or JS, CSS, and
    state passed between them. About 2–3× the code
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Criterion: UI quality
  Streamlit: Clean defaults and consistent widgets; limited control over
    layout
  FastAPI + simple frontend: Full control and potentially more polished, but
    only if time goes into styling
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Criterion: Diff view
  Streamlit: Fine: small styled HTML for inserted and deleted words
  FastAPI + simple frontend: Fine, with full control
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Criterion: Impact table and drill-down
  Streamlit: Built in: a sortable table with row selection drives a detail
    panel
  FastAPI + simple frontend: Hand-built table, filtering and detail panel;
    straightforward with HTMX, more work with plain JS
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Criterion: Upload and editing
  Streamlit: st.file_uploader and st.text_area built in
  FastAPI + simple frontend: Multipart forms and server-side editing endpoints
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Criterion: Long LLM call
  Streamlit: Progress spinner, but the page blocks while the call runs.
    Acceptable for one engineer
  FastAPI + simple frontend: Easier to keep the page responsive, but more code
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Criterion: Report export
  Streamlit: Download button for HTML or Markdown; PDF would need an extra
    library either way
  FastAPI + simple frontend: A download endpoint; same PDF caveat
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Criterion: Testability
  Streamlit: The core is already tested independently. Streamlit's AppTest can
    drive the UI headlessly in pytest
  FastAPI + simple frontend: API endpoints are very testable with TestClient;
    frontend behaviour needs a browser tool
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Criterion: Running it as a reviewer
  Streamlit: uv run streamlit run app.py; Docker is trivial
  FastAPI + simple frontend: uv run uvicorn … plus static files; also simple
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Criterion: Main risks
  Streamlit: The page re-runs on every interaction, so results must be held in
    session state and cached, or they're recomputed. Layout limits
  FastAPI + simple frontend: More moving parts, more to debug, more time
    before anything is usable

  I left out two options:
  - Gradio: weaker for tables and drill-down.
  - A full React frontend: far beyond "a small first working version".

  Recommendation: Streamlit

  The brief values a clear, working first version that a reviewer understands
  without reading code, and Streamlit covers every UI need with built-in
  widgets. It also keeps the whole app in Python, next to the core that's
  already tested. Its main weakness, limited control over layout, matters less
  here than development speed and reliability.

  The choice is also reversible. The parsing, diff, LLM, post-check and report
  logic doesn't depend on the UI, so moving to FastAPI later would mean
  rewriting only the UI layer.

  Proposed screen layout (detail comes in step 4):
  Sidebar:  inputs (default files / upload) · analysis mode (live LLM / replay)
  · model info
  Tabs:     1. Changes   — requirement diffs + other document changes
            2. Impact    — summary counts, filterable table, detail panel for
  the selected test
            3. Edit      — edit/add/remove a requirement, then re-analyse
            4. Report    — preview + download (HTML and Markdown)

  Your choice: Streamlit (recommended) or FastAPI? Once you've decided, I'll
  move to step 4, the full plan.

❯ Proceed with the full plan for the implementation in streamlit

● Step 4: Implementation plan (Streamlit)

  Guiding choices

  - The core stays framework-independent. Everything except screen rendering
    (parsing, diff, LLM, caching, report) lives in src/vcu_impact/. The
    Streamlit app is a thin layer on top. A small CLI uses the same pipeline,
    which is how a reviewer reproduces a result without the UI and how the
    example report is produced.
  - The analysis runs only when the user clicks. A live call takes 5 s to
    minutes and uses up free-tier limits, so editing a requirement shows the new
    diff immediately, but the LLM is only called on "Analyse".
  - Every result says where it came from: live from model X, replayed from a
    recording of model X on a given date, or not analysed. Nothing is ever
    invented.

  Project structure

  src/vcu_impact/
    models.py  spec_parser.py  register_parser.py  diff.py  spec_editor.py
  (existing)
    llm.py  prompt.py  analysis.py
  (existing, prompt v3)
    pipeline.py    NEW  inputs → parsed docs → diff → analysis (cache / live /
  none)
    cache.py       NEW  content-hash keys; read/write replay recordings
    report.py      NEW  build the report data; render HTML + Markdown
    templates/     NEW  report.html.j2, report.md.j2
    cli.py         NEW  `vcu-impact analyze --baseline … --revised … --register
  … --out …`
  app/
    streamlit_app.py   NEW  page layout, sidebar, tabs
    components.py      NEW  diff rendering, status badges, impact table, detail
  panel
  data/input/          provided files (unchanged)
  data/replay/         NEW  committed recorded LLM runs (JSON), keyed by input
  hash
  reports/             NEW  example exported report (.html + .md)
  docs/                llm_evaluation.md, known_issues.md, screenshots/
  eval/                evaluation harness (exists; not part of the app)
  tests/               unit + pipeline + report + UI smoke tests
  Makefile  Dockerfile  .dockerignore  README.md

  Data flow

  baseline.docx ─┐
  revised.docx ──┼─ spec_parser ──► SpecDocument ×2 ──► diff ──► SpecDiff
  ─────────┐
  (edits) ───────┘   (spec_editor applies UI edits to the .docx first)
     │
  register.xlsx ──── register_parser (links classified against both specs) ──►
  TestRegister

     │
                            cache key = sha256(prompt version + model + LLM
  input JSON)

     ▼
              ┌── replay hit?  ─► recorded AnalysisRun ("Replayed: model, date")
  pipeline ───┼── LLM configured + user clicks Analyse ─► llm → post_check ─►
  AnalysisRun ("Live")
              └── neither ─► diff + register facts only, assessments marked "not
  analysed"

     │
                                      ImpactView (one row per test: facts +
  assessment + provenance)
                                                    │                       │
                                           Streamlit tabs           report.py →
  HTML / Markdown

  Data models (new; existing ones unchanged)

  - InputSet: file names and SHA-256 hashes of the three inputs, plus a list of
    applied UI edits. It drives caching and is printed in the report.
  - TestImpactRow: the parsed TestCase facts (links with status, values,
    previous result), its TestAssessment (or none), and provenance:
    - actions_from_code: currently fix_link;
    - the post-check warnings for that test.

    The table, detail panel and report are all built from this one model.
  - AnalysisOutcome: an optional AnalysisRun, a mode (live, replay or
    not_analysed), the model label, the prompt version, a timestamp, global
    warnings and a reason (for example "No requirement changes found").
  - ReplayEntry: a cache key, a summary of the inputs, and the AnalysisRun. Each
    entry is stored as one JSON file in data/replay/.

  LLM and prompt strategy

  - One call per analysis covering all changed requirements and all tests, using
    prompt v3 at temperature 0. The output is validated against the strict JSON
    schema, with one repair retry, then post-checked:
    - coverage of all 12 tests;
    - invented IDs;
    - approval wording;
    - fix_link set by code;
    - the rewording contradiction from known_issues.md.
  - Groq qwen3.8-27b is the primary model, with optional fallback to Gemini 3.5
    Flash. The fallback is configured through LLM_FALLBACK_PROVIDER and
    LLM_FALLBACK_MODEL and is used once if the primary fails on a rate limit or
    provider error. The result always names the model that actually answered.
  - No changes, no call. If no requirement changed, there is no LLM call; the UI
    says so and still shows the other document changes.
  - Replay recordings. Prompt v3 has already been run with qwen3.8-27b on all 7
    scenarios, so those runs become the replay set. A reviewer without a key can
    then see real LLM output for the original revision and six further changes.
    They're labelled "recorded during evaluation on 2026-11-16 to 17". I'll
    verify in M1 that the app's cache keys match those runs exactly.
  - Prompt changes need a new version. A prompt change means a new version, a
    new cache key and an evaluation re-run before use.
  - Size limit. The prompt is about 3.5k tokens for 12 tests. I'll document a
    soft limit of about 60 tests per register; beyond that the UI warns, and
    batching would be needed but is out of scope.

  UI (Streamlit)

  - Sidebar:
    - Inputs: default files or upload a baseline, revised spec and register.
    - Analysis: provider and model, key present yes/no (never its value), mode
      badge.
    - Analyse button.
    - Input hashes, shown small.
  - Tab 1, Changes:
    - one card per changed requirement: old vs new with word-level highlighting,
      numeric changes (100 ms → 80 ms) and the LLM's interpretation of the
      change ("tightened") when available;
    - other document changes (revision label, revision notes) in a section that
      can be expanded;
    - unchanged requirements listed.
  - Tab 2, Impact:
    - summary counts by status and by action;
    - a filterable table (status, confidence, actions, link status, previous
      result);
    - selecting a row opens a detail panel with the reason, evidence,
      recommended action, actions labelled as coming from the model or from the
      register data, warnings, and the raw register fields;
    - the "recommendation, not approval" notice at the top.
  - Tab 3, Edit and re-analyse:
    - pick a requirement and edit its text or title, add a new requirement (ID,
      title, text, position) or remove one;
    - edits apply to the revised spec through spec_editor, so they go through
      the same parser as uploaded files;
    - a list of applied edits, a reset button, and the edited .docx can be
      downloaded.
  - Tab 4, Report: preview, with download buttons for HTML and Markdown.

  Report (HTML + Markdown)

  The report contains:
  - Header: system, revisions, input file names and hashes, model, mode (live or
    replayed), prompt version, date.
  - Disclaimer: these are recommendations based on register evidence; final
    approval rests with the authorised owner.
  - Summary of changes: with the LLM's interpretation.
  - Impact summary and work list by action: tests to re-run, procedures to
    update, links to fix, owner decisions.
  - Per-test table: status, confidence, actions and reason for all 12 tests,
    including those not affected.
  - Warnings and traceability gaps.
  - Method and limitations appendix: links to known issues.

  The HTML is self-contained and prints cleanly to PDF from the browser. I'm not
  adding a PDF library, because those pull in system dependencies that
  complicate Windows and Docker.

  Test strategy (all offline, no network, no keys)

  ┌────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Area      │                         Tests                          │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Core (exists)  │ Parsing, diff, editor, post-check: 13 tests            │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ A fake LLM client returning canned output; mode        │
  │ Pipeline       │ selection between replay, live and not analysed; the   │
  │                │ no-changes case; fallback triggered on a simulated     │
  │                │ rate limit                                             │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Same inputs give the same key; any edit or prompt      │
  │ Cache          │ change gives a new key; the committed replay files     │
  │                │ match the default inputs and the 7 scenarios           │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Required sections present; all tests listed;           │
  │ Report         │ disclaimer present; no approval wording; the S5-style  │
  │                │ contradiction warning appears                          │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ UI smoke       │ The app loads in replay mode with the default files    │
  │ (Streamlit     │ and shows 12 rows. Applying an edit updates the diff.  │
  │ AppTest)       │ The report download is available                       │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM quality    │ Stays in eval/ (needs keys, not part of make test)     │
  └────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Milestones (each one: tests pass, I run it, commit)

  1. M1, pipeline: pipeline, cache, CLI, and replay files converted from the v3
     evaluation runs. Check: vcu-impact analyze reproduces the S1 result in
     replay mode.
  2. M2, read-only UI: sidebar, Changes tab, Impact tab with the detail panel. I
     run it and check it headlessly.
  3. M3, edits and live runs: editing, uploads, the Analyse button with live
     Groq, and the fallback. Check: a real live run on a new edit, such as DR-01
     changed to 90 ms.
  4. M4, report: HTML and Markdown output, the Report tab, and the example
     report committed in reports/.
  5. M5, packaging and deliverables:
     - Makefile and Dockerfile;
     - README: purpose, exact commands, model configuration, reproducing a
       result, assumptions, known issues, links;
     - screenshots;
     - final secrets and .gitignore check.

  Decisions I need from you

  1. Report formats: HTML and Markdown, with PDF through the browser's print
     function. Is that acceptable, or do you want a generated PDF?
  2. Screenshots: should I generate them automatically with Playwright? That's a
     dev dependency plus a headless browser download of about 150 MB, but
     screenshots can then be regenerated. Or would you rather take them
     yourself?
  3. Docker: it isn't installed here, so I can write the Dockerfile but can't
     verify it. Will you install Docker (Docker Desktop with WSL integration, or
     Docker Engine inside WSL) so I can test it? Otherwise I'll mark it "not
     verified" in the README.
  4. Pushing: should I push after each milestone, or only when you ask?

  Once you've approved the plan and answered these, I'll save it as docs/plan.md
  and start M1.

● The word diff looks unreadable when a paragraph is heavily rewritten, since it
  interleaves single deleted/inserted words. I'll fix diff.py by merging change
  runs separated only by whitespace, and showing old/new versions as separate
  blocks when most of the text changed. · summary

        1  # VCU-42 Spec Change Impact Analyzer
        2 
        3 -> Work in progress. This README will be completed once the app
          -lication is built.                                            
        4 -                                                              
        3  AI-assisted impact analysis for a fictional vehicle controller
            (VCU-42): compare two
        4  specification revisions, find the existing tests that may be a
           ffected, review the LLM's
        5  explanation, and export an engineering impact summary.
        6 
        7 +- **What changed:** requirement-level diff of two Word specifi
          +cations, with word-level                                      
        8 +  highlighting and numeric changes (e.g. `100 milliseconds → 8
          +0 milliseconds`).                                             
        9 +- **Which tests may be affected, and why:** every test in the 
          +Excel register gets a                                         
       10 +  status, a confidence, required actions (re-run, update proce
          +dure, fix link, owner                                         
       11 +  decision) and a short evidence-based reason, including tests
          + judged *not affected*.                                       
       12 +- **Try a further change:** edit, add or remove a requirement 
          +in the browser, or upload                                     
       13 +  other files, then re-run the analysis.                      
       14 +- **Export:** an HTML or Markdown engineering impact report fr
          +om the UI or the CLI.                                         
       15 +                                                              
       16 +The register is treated as **evidence, not approval**. The app
          +lication gives                                                
       17 +recommendations; final approval belongs to the authorized owne
          +r.                                                            
       18 +                                                              
       19 +> **Sample report:** [reports/example_impact_report.html](repo
          +rts/example_impact_report.html)                               
       20 +> (also as [Markdown](reports/example_impact_report.md)), gene
          +rated from the provided files.                                
       21 +                                                              
       22 +<!-- TODO(owner): add the screenshots, e.g. docs/screenshots/c
          +hanges.png and impact.png -->                                 
       23 +**Screenshots:** _to be added in `docs/screenshots/`._        
       24 +                                                              
       25 +## Quick start                                                
       26 +                                                              
       27 +Requirements: [uv](https://docs.astral.sh/uv/) (it installs Py
          +thon 3.12 if needed). No API                                  
       28 +key is needed to try the application: without one, it shows **
          +recorded** LLM analyses                                       
       29 +(see [What is live and what is replayed](#what-is-live-and-wha
          +t-is-replayed)).                                              
       30 +                                                              
       31 +### Ubuntu / WSL / macOS                                      
       32 +                                                              
       33 +```bash                                                       
       34 +curl -LsSf https://astral.sh/uv/install.sh | sh     # once; th
          +en open a new terminal                                        
       35 +git clone <repository-url> vcu-impact-analyzer && cd vcu-impac
          +t-analyzer                                                    
       36 +make install    # uv sync + creates .env from .env.example    
       37 +make run        # http://localhost:8501                       
       38 +```                                                           
       39 +                                                              
       40 +Without `make`: `uv sync`, `cp .env.example .env`, `uv run str
          +eamlit run app/streamlit_app.py`.                             
       41 +                                                              
       42 +### Windows (PowerShell)                                      
       43 +                                                              
       44 +```powershell                                                 
       45 +powershell -ExecutionPolicy ByPass -c "irm https://astral.sh/u
          +v/install.ps1 | iex"   # once; then open a new terminal       
       46 +git clone <repository-url> vcu-impact-analyzer; cd vcu-impact-
          +analyzer                                                      
       47 +uv sync                                                       
       48 +Copy-Item .env.example .env                                   
       49 +uv run streamlit run app/streamlit_app.py                     
       50 +```                                                           
       51 +                                                              
       52 +> **Not verified on native Windows.** Development and testing 
          +were done on Ubuntu 24.04                                     
       53 +> (WSL2). The code uses only cross-platform Python packages an
          +d `pathlib`, but these                                        
       54 +> PowerShell commands have not been run on a Windows machine. 
       55 +                                                              
       56 +### Docker                                                    
       57 +

       59 +docker build -t vcu-impact .                                  
       60 +docker run --rm -p 8501:8501 vcu-impact                    # r
          +eplay mode, no key                                            
       61 +docker run --rm -p 8501:8501 --env-file .env vcu-impact    # w
          +ith a live model                                              
       62 +```                                                           
       63 +                                                              
       64 +Then open http://localhost:8501. `make docker-build` and `make
          + docker-run` do the same.                                     
       65 +The image never contains `.env` (excluded by `.dockerignore`);
          + keys are passed at run time.                                 
       66 +                                                              
       67 +<!-- TODO: update once the image has been built and run. -->
